[飞书用户 用户]: 📊 论文自检任务：图表数据与数字一致性检查

请对当前论文做一次数据正确性与一致性自检。AI 写作另一个高频问题：图表里的数字、正文里引用的数字、Abstract/Conclusion 里的数字三者经常对不上，或者跟实验日志里的真实结果有出入。

自检要求：

1. 图表正确性
   - 每一张表格、每一张图：数据来源是什么？是从 results.yaml / 实验日志 / WandB 拉的，还是手填的？
   - 表头、列名、单位、有效位数是否正确？
   - 图的坐标轴标签、图例、caption 描述与图内容是否一致？
   - 公式（equations）符号定义是否在首次出现时给出？符号是否前后一致（同一个变量没用两个不同符号）？

2. 数字一致性（critical）
   - Abstract 里的关键数字（main result、improvement %、scale）
   - Introduction 里 contribution 部分的数字
   - Method 里描述的超参（learning rate、batch size、训练步数）
   - 主表（main result table）的数字
   - Analysis 里引用的数字
   - Conclusion 里复述的数字
   这 6 处提到的同一个指标必须完全一致（精度也要一致：Abstract 写 73.2% 就不能在表格里是 73.18%、Conclusion 里又变成 73%）。

3. 与实验真值对账
   - 主结果表格的数字，是否能在实验目录的 results.yaml / 日志里找到对应原始记录？
   - 如果做过 cherry-pick（选了最好的 seed），是否在文中说明了 reporting protocol（best of N / mean ± std / median）？
   - 有没有还在用占位（XX.X、TBD、---）的数字？必须全部填实或明确标注 "results pending"。

修完后请简短汇报：(a) 改了哪几张表/图的数字，(b) 一致性问题修在了哪几个章节，(c) 还有哪些数字是占位待补。

```text
Perfect! Now I have all the main content files. Let me compile the complete report with all numbers, statistics, and metrics found:

## Complete Report: Depth Profiling Paper Contents and Metrics

### FILE 1: main.tex (First 80 lines - Abstract extraction point)
The document is formatted for EMNLP 2026 ARR Submission using ACL 2023 template. It includes sections on introduction, related work, method, results, discussion, conclusion, and limitations, plus multiple appendices.

---

### FILE 2: abstract.tex (FULL FILE)
**Title:** "Depth Profiling: Classifying Recursive Generation Depth in Base-Model LLM Text"

**Key metrics from abstract:**
- **Effect sizes (Cohen's d):**
  - Location features: d = 0.74–1.05 (monotonic drift with depth)
  - Shape features: d < 0.10 (near-stationary)
  - Entropy std: d = 0.46 (intermediate)
  - Effect size gap: ≥8× between location and shape

- **Performance metrics:**
  - Leak-free binary F1 = 0.694 (gen-1 vs. gen-2+)
  - Cross-model binary F1 = 0.830
  - 3-class F1 = 0.762 (under leave-one-generator-out evaluation)

- **Dataset note:** gen-0 seeds shared across generators (controlled)

- **Key finding:** Instruction tuning compresses DDR, limiting applicability to base models

---

### FILE 3: method.tex (FULL FILE)

**Table 1: Seven Distributional Features**
Features extract from NLL distributions under Pythia-410M reference model:

**NLL-based features:**
- entropy_mean (measures central tendency of token surprise)
- entropy_std (spread of token surprise)
- entropy_max (maximum token surprise)
- entropy_min (minimum token surprise)
- tail_decay (log-prob decay slope, ranks 100–1000)

**Surface features:**
- ttr (type-token ratio)
- hapax_ratio (proportion of hapax legomena)

**Generation setup:**
- **Number of generators:** 8 total
- **Model families:** 5 (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen, Llama, Mistral)
- **Generator specifications:**
  - Pythia: 2.8B, 1B
  - OLMo: OLMo-2-1B
  - Qwen: Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base
  - Llama: Llama-3.2-3B
  - Mistral: Mistral-7B-v0.3
- **Parameter range:** 13× (0.6B–8B)
- **Qwen overrepresentation:** 3/8 generators
- **LOFO performance:** F1 = 0.686 (with Qwen overrepresentation acknowledged)

**Dataset construction:**
- **Seed passages:** approximately 1,000 from English Wikipedia per generator
- **Chain depth:** depth 4 (gen-0 through gen-4)
- **Prompt:** "Continue the following text: {text}"
- **Sampling parameters:**
  - Temperature τ = 0.7
  - Nucleus sampling p = 0.9
  - Maximum length: 256 new tokens
- **5-class analysis result:** F1 ≈ 0.27 (adjacent depths 2–4 indistinguishable, motivating gen-2+ collapse)
- **Final dataset size:** 37,058 texts across all depths
- **Depths 2–4:** Collapsed into 2+ class

**Feature extraction details:**
- Reference model: Pythia-410M (smaller than all generators)
- **Feature redundancy screening:** 6 additional NLL statistics rejected (|r| > 0.65, ΔF1 ≤ −0.011)
- **Cross-family reference validation:** F1 spread = 0.013 (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen references)
- **Feature correlation:** TTR and hapax ratio correlation r = 0.97 (strongly correlated, retained for marginal LOFO gains)

**Evaluation protocol:**
- **Primary metric:** Macro-averaged F1 over three depth classes
- **Confidence intervals:** 95% bootstrap (n = 1,000)
- **Bonferroni correction:** Applied within each family of simultaneous tests
- **Classifier:** XGBoost
  - n_estimators = 200
  - max_depth = 6
  - learning rate = 0.1
  - Hyperparameter sensitivity: F1 variation ≤ 2.0 pp

**Baselines compared:**
1. Avg-LogProb
2. FastDetectGPT
3. Binoculars
4. LD-Score

---

### FILE 4: results.tex (FULL FILE)

**Figure: Cross-generator 8×8 transfer matrix (binary F1, gen-1 vs. gen-2+)**
- **Mean cross-generator F1:** 0.810
- **95% CI:** [0.796, 0.822]
- Uniform high off-diagonal values confirm depth signal transfers across five families

**Classification Performance (Section 3-class LOGO):**
- **8-generator LOGO F1:** 0.762
- **95% CI:** [0.758, 0.766]
- **Dataset size N:** 37,058
- **Cross-generator F1 spread:** 9.1 pp
- **Per-class gen-0 F1:** ≈0.94 (shared gen-0 seeds bias)
- **Leak-free binary F1** (gen-1 vs. gen-2+, excluding gen-0): 0.694
- **Binary F1 CI:** [0.688, 0.700]

**Per-generator binary F1 results:**
- Pythia-2.8B: 0.724 [.707, .742] – highest; 3-class: 0.798
- Pythia-1B: 0.718 [.700, .735]; 3-class: 0.806 (best 3-class)
- Qwen3-1.7B: 0.709 [.693, .726]; 3-class: 0.772
- Llama-3.2-3B: 0.697 [.680, .713]; 3-class: 0.775
- OLMo-2-1B: 0.695 [.679, .711]; 3-class: 0.781
- Qwen3-8B: 0.692 [.674, .708]; 3-class: 0.714
- Qwen3-0.6B: 0.691 [.673, .709]; 3-class: 0.780
- Mistral-7B: 0.633 [.617, .648] – lowest; 3-class: 0.682
- **Per-generator std. dev.:** 0.028
- **Primary difficulty:** gen-1/gen-2+ boundary

**Cross-model generalization:**
- **Cross-model chains binary F1:** 0.830
- **95% CI:** [.818, .841]
- **LOGO setup:** 5-generator (Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B)
- **Single-generator transfer mean F1:** 0.810 (Figure 1)
- **LOFO (family-level):** F1 = 0.686
- **ANOVA generator variance:** η²_gen ≤ 0.009 (<1% of feature variance)
- **Model size effect:**
  - Pythia-2.8B: 0.724 (smaller models perform better)
  - Mistral-7B: 0.633 (larger model lower)
  - Same-family affinity with Pythia-410M reference spread ≤ 0.013

**End-to-end neural classifier (QLoRA):**
- **3-class F1:** 0.905
- **Improvement:** +10.0 pp over distributional features
- **Setup:** Within-model 5-fold CV
- **On instruct data:** QLoRA recovers to 0.89–0.91 (within-model)

**Confound controls:**
- **Length-matched subsampling binary F1:** 0.724 (vs. 0.770 without matching)
- **Independent-seed evaluation (disjoint pools):** F1 = 0.680

**Error analysis:**
- **Depth-2 error rate (highest):** 48.4%
- **Depth-4 error rate:** 20.3% (monotonic decrease)
- **Depth-3+ error rates:** ≤29.2%
- **Primary failure:** gen-1/gen-2+ boundary

**Differential Depth Response (DDR) Definition:**
- **Depth sensitivity:** DDR(f) = |ρ(depth, f)| using Spearman correlation
- **Drifting threshold:** Cohen's d > 0.50 between adjacent depths
- **Stationary threshold:** d < 0.15
- **k-means silhouette score:** 0.660
- **Silhouette p-value:** 0.030
- **Robustness range:** ε ∈ [0.10, 0.75]

**Feature drift patterns:**
- **Drifting features (Spearman |ρ|):**
  - hapax_ratio: ρ = -0.64
  - TTR: ρ = -0.62
  - entropy_mean: ρ = -0.60
- **Near-stationary features:**
  - entropy_max: |ρ| ≤ 0.16
  - tail_decay: |ρ| ≤ 0.16
  - entropy_min: |ρ| = 0.40 (boundary feature, sensitive at gen-0/gen-1)
- **Magnitude gap:** ≥8×

**Cohen's d effect sizes:**
- **Drifting features:** d = 0.74–1.05
- **Near-stationary features:** d < 0.10
- **Gap factor:** ≥8×
- **Entropy std:** d = 0.46 (intermediate)

**Boundary feature analysis:**
- **Entropy min:** d = 0.016 (near-stationary for gen-1/gen-2+)
- **Tail decay TOST equivalence:** 4/8 generators (|d| = 0.10–0.22)

**Validation statistics:**
- **Per-generator hold-out silhouette:** 0.505–0.760
- **TOST entropy min:** 8/8 generators confirmed near-stationary
- **TOST entropy max:** 6/8 generators confirmed
- **Permutation testing p-value:** 0.030 (partition non-random)

**Feature complementarity ablations:**
- **Full 7-feature set (10-fold stratified CV):** F1 = 0.773
- **Surface-only:** F1 = 0.688
- **Reference-only:** F1 = 0.713
- **McNemar p-value:** ≈ 0
- **3-class F1 gains from non-drifting features:** +5.3 pp
  - Drifting-only: F1 = 0.716
  - All-7: F1 = 0.769
- **Binary detection (drifting-only):** F1 = 0.697
- **Binary detection (all-7):** F1 = 0.700 (already saturated)

**Cross-family reference models:**
- **Alternative references F1 spread:** 0.013 (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B)
- **Same-family Pythia F1 inflation:** Up to 1.2 pp

**Temperature and prompt sensitivity:**

- **Continuation chains F1:** 0.781 (strongest drift)
- **Random word replacement (10%) degradation:** −8 pp binary F1

**Baseline comparison (8-gen LOGO, pooled):**

  - 3-class F1: 0.795

  - Inference: 22.8 ms/text
- **4-detector ensemble (4 features):**

1	% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	As large language model (LLM) outputs re-enter training corpora and seed further generation, understanding how recursive generation reshapes text distributions is critical for data provenance and corpus curation---yet existing detectors answer only the binary human-or-machine question, ignoring \emph{generation depth}: we introduce \emph{depth profiling} to address this gap.
4	We decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, negative log-likelihood (NLL) location features drift monotonically with depth (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.46$)---a ${\geq}8{\times}$ effect-size gap.
5	Prior work documents \emph{what converges}; the central finding here is \emph{what is approximately preserved}: shape stability complements location drift for ordinal depth grading without per-generator calibration, achieving leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+), cross-model binary F1\,=\,0.830, and 3-class F1\,=\,0.762 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation (gen-0 seeds shared across generators; \S\ref{sec:generation}).
6	Instruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models.
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```

1	\vspace{-1mm}
2	\section{Method}
3	\label{sec:method}
4	
5	\begin{table}[t!]
6	\centering
7	\footnotesize
8	\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (\S\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals entropy mean, TTR, and hapax ratio drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate.}
9	\label{tab:features}
10	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
11	\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{3.0cm}@{}}
12	\toprule
13	\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Measures} \\
14	\midrule
15	\texttt{entropy\_mean} \textit{\scriptsize(NLL)} & Ref.\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\
16	\texttt{entropy\_std} \textit{\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\
17	\texttt{entropy\_max} \textit{\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\
18	\texttt{entropy\_min} \textit{\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\
19	\texttt{tail\_decay}   & Ref.\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\
20	\midrule
21	\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\
22	\texttt{hapax\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\
23	\bottomrule
24	\end{tabular}
25	\end{table}
26	
27	\begin{figure*}[t]
28	\centering
29	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig2_framework.pdf}
30	\caption{Depth profiling pipeline. (a)~Human seeds are iteratively fed through generator $G$ to produce chains of increasing depth. (b)~Seven features extracted from NLL distributions group into three DDR zones: drifting (red), intermediate (purple), near-stationary (blue). (c)~LOGO evaluation holds out one generator for testing.}
31	\label{fig:framework}
32	\end{figure*}
33	
34	\subsection{Task Formulation}
35	\label{sec:task}
36	
37	Given a text $x$, we predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).
38	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.
39	Depths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
40	
41	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
42	\label{sec:generation}
43	
44	\looseness=-1 We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}, \textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \cite{llama3}, and \textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \cite{mistral7b}.
45	\looseness=-1 These span a 13$\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B) across five architectures; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8) is a limitation mitigated by LOFO (F1\,=\,0.686; \S\ref{sec:results-classification}).
46	
47	\looseness=-1 For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
48	The same seed pool serves as gen-0 for all generators, controlling for input content; in LOGO evaluation this creates a potential train--test overlap for gen-0, addressed by our binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
49	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
50	We generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\,$\approx$\,0.27; Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.
51	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
52	After filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.
53	
54	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
55	\label{sec:features}
56	
57	\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.
58	
59	We use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$10.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition (LOFO confirms both feature groups contribute independently; Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
60	
61	\looseness=-1 We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\ref{tab:features}), covering the NLL distribution's location, spread, extremes, and tail structure, plus two reference-free surface diversity measures; six additional NLL statistics were rejected for redundancy ($|r| > 0.65$, $\Delta$F1\,$\leq$\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\ref{app:feature-candidate}).
62	
63	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
64	\looseness=-1 A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
65	Cross-family reference validation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.
66	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable.
67	
68	\paragraph{Surface features.}
69	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\_ratio).
70	
71	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
72	These features exhibit a \emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).
73	TTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.
74	
75	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
76	\label{sec:evaluation}
77	
78	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
79	\looseness=-1 We evaluate generalization via \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.
80	LOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\,=\,0.686; \S\ref{sec:results-classification}).
81	Generator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).
82	
83	\paragraph{Classifier.}
84	\looseness=-1 We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}.
85	Alternative classifiers (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}); hyperparameter sensitivity (max\_depth sweep) shows F1 variation $\leq$2.0\,pp, confirming discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
86	
87	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
88	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $n{=}1{,}000$).
89	All $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.
90	
91	\paragraph{Baselines.}
92	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
93	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.
94	
95	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
96	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
97

  "toolUseResult": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/method.tex",
      "content": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{table}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (\\S\\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals entropy mean, TTR, and hapax ratio drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate.}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{3.0cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Source} & \\textbf{Measures} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{entropy\\_mean} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)} & Ref.\\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_std} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_max} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_min} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{tail\\_decay}   & Ref.\\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\\\\n\\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig2_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Depth profiling pipeline. (a)~Human seeds are iteratively fed through generator $G$ to produce chains of increasing depth. (b)~Seven features extracted from NLL distributions group into three DDR zones: drifting (red), intermediate (purple), near-stationary (blue). (c)~LOGO evaluation holds out one generator for testing.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, we predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\n\\looseness=-1 We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}, \\textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \\cite{llama3}, and \\textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \\cite{mistral7b}.\n\\looseness=-1 These span a 13$\\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B) across five architectures; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8) is a limitation mitigated by LOFO (F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same seed pool serves as gen-0 for all generators, controlling for input content; in LOGO evaluation this creates a potential train--test overlap for gen-0, addressed by our binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nWe generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.27; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nAfter filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.\n\nWe use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$10.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition (LOFO confirms both feature groups contribute independently; Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\\ref{tab:features}), covering the NLL distribution's location, spread, extremes, and tail structure, plus two reference-free surface diversity measures; six additional NLL statistics were rejected for redundancy ($|r| > 0.65$, $\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\n\\looseness=-1 A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nCross-family reference validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\n\\looseness=-1 We evaluate generalization via \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.\nLOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\n\\looseness=-1 We use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); hyperparameter sensitivity (max\\_depth sweep) shows F1 variation $\\leq$2.0\\,pp, confirming discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",

31	The corresponding visualization appears in Figure~\ref{fig:variance-decomp} (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
32	
33	The depth factor explains 34--37\% of location-feature variance but $<$1\% of shape-feature variance---a ${>}48\times$ ratio that quantifies the DDR partition as a systematic depth$\times$feature-type interaction rather than a post-hoc grouping.
34	Generator identity contributes comparably across both groups ($\eta^2_{\text{gen}} {=} 0.02$), confirming that the asymmetry is specific to depth, not generator.
35	Interaction terms are uniformly small ($\eta^2_{\text{int}} < 0.011$), indicating that the depth effect on location features operates consistently across generators.
36	
37	Exponential convergence fitting ($f(d) {=} a \cdot e^{-\lambda d} + c$) on per-generator depth trajectories corroborates this: location features converge with moderate rates ($\lambda {=} 0.40 \pm 0.26$, $R^2 {=} 0.99$), consistent with contraction toward a generator-specific fixed point under iterated autoregressive generation, while shape features show degenerate fits ($\lambda {=} 5.3 \pm 8.4$, $R^2 {=} 0.73$) reflecting their flat trajectories.

```text
1	To assess whether depth signal extends beyond handcrafted features, we fine-tune a pretrained language model end-to-end as a depth classifier using QLoRA \cite{qlora}.
2	
3	\paragraph{Setup.}
4	We fine-tune Qwen3-0.6B-Base with LoRA rank 16, learning rate $2 \times 10^{-4}$, and 3 epochs, framing depth estimation as 5-class sequence classification (gen-0 through gen-4).\footnote{Evaluated on a 4-generator subset (Pythia-1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-1.7B). We expect 8-generator training to further improve given additional diversity; the comparison direction is preserved.}
5	For 3-class evaluation, we collapse predictions into gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ post hoc.
6	All results use 5-fold stratified cross-validation on the 4-generator subset.
7	
8	\begin{table}[ht]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\caption{QLoRA end-to-end depth classification vs.\ XGBoost on handcrafted features, under 5-fold stratified CV (4-generator subset).}
12	\label{tab:qlora}
13	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{5-class F1} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Per-class F1 (5-class)} \\
17	\midrule
18	XGBoost (7 features) & 0.497 $\pm$ 0.008 & 0.805 $\pm$ 0.008 & --- \\
19	QLoRA (Qwen3-0.6B)   & 0.583 $\pm$ 0.010 & 0.905 $\pm$ 0.003 & 0.999\,/\,0.796\,/\,0.413\,/\,0.259\,/\,0.447 \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\paragraph{Results.}
25	QLoRA achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 ($\pm$0.003) under 5-fold stratified CV on a 4-generator subset, a +10.0\,pp improvement over the same-subset XGBoost baseline (0.805).
26	On 5-class evaluation, QLoRA reaches F1\,=\,0.583 ($\pm$0.010) vs.\ 0.497 for XGBoost (+8.6\,pp), with the largest gains at gen-1 (0.796 vs.\ 0.650) and gen-2 (0.413 vs.\ 0.267).
27	The gen-2/gen-3 boundary remains the primary failure mode (gen-3 F1\,=\,0.259), consistent with the feature-level analysis in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
28	
29	\paragraph{Hyperparameter robustness.}
30	A second run with LoRA rank 32 and seed 123 yields 5-class F1\,=\,0.587 ($\pm$0.005) and 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 ($\pm$0.004), which confirms stability across hyperparameter and seed variation ($\Delta < 1$\,pp on both metrics).
31	
32	\paragraph{Implications.}
33	The substantial 3-class improvement demonstrates that raw token embeddings contain depth signal beyond what summary statistics capture; the seven handcrafted features are a strong but lossy compression of the full distributional information.
34	End-to-end fine-tuning exploits token-level patterns (e.g., positional regularities, n-gram repetitions) that aggregate features cannot represent.
35	This confirms that the depth signal documented in our main analysis is real and exploitable beyond engineered features, while the handcrafted approach retains practical advantages: interpretability, computational efficiency (no GPU inference), and the ability to reveal the differential depth response pattern that motivates the task formulation.
36	The QLoRA ceiling thus represents an information-asymmetric upper bound rather than a competing method: it consumes the full token sequence (0.6B parameters, GPU inference) whereas the 7-feature ensemble distills the signal into interpretable scalars that expose \emph{why} depth is detectable---a decomposition that end-to-end models cannot provide.
37	
```

</details>

1	This appendix reports per-generator breakdowns for the cross-model chain evaluation described in \S\ref{sec:results-classification}.
2	
3	\begin{table}[ht]
4	\centering
5	\footnotesize
6	\caption{Cross-model chain per-class performance (generator-level leave-one-out, $N$\,=\,2{,}964, $n_{\text{generators}}$\,=\,5: Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B). Macro F1\,=\,0.831 under class balancing; under natural class distribution, macro F1\,=\,0.789. Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841], case-level bootstrap, $n$\,=\,2{,}000). Cross-model chains avoid the gen-0 seed sharing issue present in single-generator LOGO.}
7	\label{tab:cross-model-perclass}
8	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
9	\toprule
10	\textbf{Class} & \textbf{Prec.} & \textbf{Rec.} & \textbf{F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
11	\midrule
12	gen-0   & 0.921 & 0.948 & 0.935 & [.923, .946] \\
13	gen-1   & 0.737 & 0.803 & 0.768 & [.748, .788] \\
14	gen-2+  & 0.842 & 0.742 & 0.789 & [.768, .809] \\
15	\midrule
16	\textbf{Macro} & --- & --- & \textbf{0.831} & [.817, .844] \\
17	\bottomrule
18	\end{tabular}
19	\end{table}
20	
21	\paragraph{Variance decomposition.}
22	A two-way ANOVA (generator $\times$ depth) on cross-model chain features ($n{=}3{,}778$, depths 1--4) confirms that generator identity contributes negligible variance: partial $\eta^2_{\text{gen}} < 1\%$ for all seven features (range 0.05\%--0.90\%, mean 0.37\%), while depth accounts for 15.3\%--29.9\% of variance in drifting features.
23	The generator$\times$depth interaction is also $< 1\%$.
24	This confirms that the F1\,=\,0.830 cross-model advantage reflects depth signal rather than generator diversity accumulation: mixing generators across chain steps does not introduce a confounding generator-identity cue.
25	
```

1	We evaluate finer-grained 5-class depth classification (gen-0 through gen-4) using the same 7-feature ensemble and LOGO protocol described in \S\ref{sec:method}.
2	
3	\begin{table}[ht]
4	\centering
5	\small
6	\caption{5-class LOGO evaluation. Each row holds out one generator for testing, training on the remaining five.}
7	\label{tab:5class-logo}
8	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
9	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
10	\toprule
11	\textbf{Held-out Generator} & \textbf{Macro F1} & \textbf{Weighted F1} & \textbf{Accuracy} \\
12	\midrule
13	OLMo-2-1B       & 0.484 & 0.506 & 0.524 \\
14	Pythia-1B        & 0.543 & 0.568 & 0.575 \\
15	Pythia-2.8B      & 0.523 & 0.540 & 0.560 \\
16	Qwen3-0.6B      & 0.513 & 0.521 & 0.546 \\
17	Qwen3-1.7B      & 0.510 & 0.536 & 0.548 \\
18	Qwen3-8B        & 0.434 & 0.455 & 0.493 \\
19	\midrule
20	\textbf{Average} & \textbf{0.501 $\pm$ 0.038} & \textbf{0.521} & \textbf{0.541} \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	\begin{table}[ht]
26	\centering
27	\small
28	\caption{Per-class F1 averaged across all six LOGO folds.}
29	\label{tab:5class-perclass}
30	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
31	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
32	\toprule
33	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Gen-0} & \textbf{Gen-1} & \textbf{Gen-2} & \textbf{Gen-3} & \textbf{Gen-4} \\
34	\midrule
35	Avg F1      & 0.921 & 0.634 & 0.268 & 0.259 & 0.423 \\
36	\bottomrule
37	\end{tabular}}
38	\end{table}
39	
40	\paragraph{Analysis.}
41	Moving from 3-class (gen-0 / gen-1 / gen-2+) to 5-class classification reduces LOGO macro F1 to approximately 0.50 (six-generator subset; the 3-class baseline for this subset is comparable to the 8-generator result of 0.762 in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
42	The degradation is concentrated in the middle depths: gen-2 and gen-3 achieve only 0.268 and 0.259 F1, respectively.
43	The aggregated confusion matrix reveals that 36.5\% of true gen-2 samples are misclassified as gen-1, while 37.8\% of true gen-3 samples are predicted as gen-4.
44	Adjacent depths (gen-2$\leftrightarrow$gen-3, gen-3$\leftrightarrow$gen-4) account for the majority of errors, consistent with the intuition that deeper recursive generations converge in surface-level statistical signatures.
45	Gen-0 (human text) remains highly distinguishable (F1 = 0.921), and gen-1 retains moderate separability (F1 = 0.634).
46	Shape features that are near-stationary in the 3-class setting become substantially more important under 5-class evaluation: permutation importance of entropy\_std increases by 93\% and tail\_decay by 400\% relative to 3-class, yet these gains are insufficient to resolve adjacent depths.
47	These results support the 3-class grouping adopted in the main paper: collapsing gen-2+ preserves the most informative boundary (human vs.\ first-generation vs.\ deeper recursion) while avoiding the noise introduced by near-identical adjacent depths.
48	
49	\paragraph{Classifier comparison.}
50	To verify that 5-class degradation reflects feature-level limitations rather than classifier choice, we evaluate four alternatives under the same protocol: HistGradientBoosting (macro F1\,=\,0.501, 95\% bootstrap CI [0.496, 0.506]), random forest (0.476), logistic regression (0.447), and Frank-Hall ordinal reduction (0.451).
51	All classifiers perform comparably to the default XGBoost ensemble (0.501), indicating that classifier capacity is not the bottleneck.
52	The gen-2/gen-3 boundary is the primary failure mode across all methods: these two classes are effectively inseparable with the current feature set. This establishes 3-class granularity as the practical ceiling for surface and entropy features.
53	At the 3-class level, ordinal-specific methods (CORAL ordinal regression, Frank-Hall reduction) similarly fail to improve over multiclass XGBoost (\S\ref{sec:results}), which confirms that the bottleneck is feature separability across all granularities, not the modeling of label ordinality.
54	
```

I'm checking the confusion matrix data more carefully—gen-2 and gen-3 are genuinely indistinguishable with F1 scores around 0.26-0.27, but the adjacent misclassifications show gen-2 samples getting confused with gen-1 while gen-3 samples drift toward gen-4, which explains why collapsing gen-2+ makes sense despite gen-4 having a higher F1 score of 0.423. I'm settling on a cleaner phrasing that emphasizes the core issue—depths 2 through 4 are essentially indistinguishable from each other, which is why they get collapsed in the analysis. The macro F1 of 0.50 shows reasonable overall performance, but those specific classes have very poor separation, so I'll keep it concise and let the appendix handle the granular details.

Now I'm catching an inconsistency in the conclusion section. The text claims generator identity contributes around 2%, but that's from the full-dataset ANOVA in the appendix. The cross-model ANOVA reported in Results shows it's actually less than 1%, so I need to update that line to match what's in Results. Though I notice the conclusion also references a ">48×" figure, which does come from the full-dataset ANOVA, so I need to be careful about which numbers belong where. The cross-model ANOVA gives me different numbers—depth ranges from 15.3% to 29.9% for drifting features with generator under 1%, which would only yield a 15× ratio rather than >48×. So I can't mix those statistics. Option D works best: I'll attribute the specific numbers directly to the full-dataset ANOVA with a clear appendix reference, making it explicit which analysis supports which claim.

But now I'm noticing a direct conflict between the Results section stating generator identity contributes <1% and the Conclusion saying ≈2%—a reviewer would definitely catch that inconsistency. The priority is fixing this contradiction.

Since the full-dataset ANOVA (N=37,058) is more comprehensive than the subset (N=3,778), the ≈2% figure is better supported, but Results is the main text and more prominent, so I should align the Conclusion with Results by changing it to <1%. I also need to verify that the >48× variance ratio attribution in the Discussion is properly sourced and doesn't have similar issues. with the most defensible approach: keeping the Results section's cross-model finding (<1%) and the Conclusion's full-dataset result (≈2%) since they're drawn from different analyses and both are correctly cited. The Results text already specifies it's from the cross-model analysis, so there's no actual inconsistency—just two separate ANOVA results from different scopes.

The real issue is that readers will see these percentages side-by-side and assume they contradict each other. I could resolve this by rephrasing the Conclusion to use a statement that holds true for both analyses, like "generator identity contributes ≤2%"—but that still reads as different from <1%. A cleaner approach is to remove the cross-model specificity from the Conclusion statement entirely, letting it stand as the broader full-dataset finding without implying it applies to the cross-model chains specifically. Now I have the full picture. The `appendix_finer_depth.tex` shows LOGO 5-class macro F1 = 0.501, with per-class gen-2 F1 = 0.268 and gen-3 F1 = 0.259. The Method's "F1 ≈ 0.27" is actually the gen-2 per-class F1 presented misleadingly as a macro F1. Let me fix the identified inconsistencies.

**Issues found:**

1. **CRITICAL**: Method says "5-class F1 ≈ 0.27" but the appendix macro F1 = 0.50 — the 0.27 is per-class gen-2 F1 (0.268) presented without qualification. Discussion/Limitations correctly say "F1 ≈ 0.50".

2. **CRITICAL**: Conclusion says "generator identity contributes ≈2%" (full-dataset ANOVA) but Results says "<1%" (cross-model ANOVA). Different analyses, but creates apparent conflict for the reader.

Fixing both now.

(a)~Human seeds are iteratively fed through generator $G$ to produce chains of increasing depth. (b)~Seven features extracted from NLL distributions group into three DDR zones: drifting (red), intermediate (purple), near-stationary (blue). (c)~LOGO evaluation holds out one generator for testing.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, we predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\n\\looseness=-1 We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}, \\textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \\cite{llama3}, and \\textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \\cite{mistral7b}.\n\\looseness=-1 These span a 13$\\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B) across five architectures; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8) is a limitation mitigated by LOFO (F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same seed pool serves as gen-0 for all generators, controlling for input content; in LOGO evaluation this creates a potential train--test overlap for gen-0, addressed by our binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nWe generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.27; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nAfter filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.\n\nWe use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$10.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition (LOFO confirms both feature groups contribute independently; Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\\ref{tab:features}), covering the NLL distribution's location, spread, extremes, and tail structure, plus two reference-free surface diversity measures; six additional NLL statistics were rejected for redundancy ($|r| > 0.65$, $\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\n\\looseness=-1 A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nCross-family reference validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\n\\looseness=-1 We evaluate generalization via \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.\nLOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\n\\looseness=-1 We use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); hyperparameter sensitivity (max\\_depth sweep) shows F1 variation $\\leq$2.0\\,pp, confirming discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",

```text
Excellent. Now let me compile my findings from the files I've read:

## Key Numbers Report from Appendix Files

**1. appendix_prompt_type.tex** - Continuation Chains F1 Scores
- **Continuation (prompt-based)**: 0.78 macro-F1 (5-fold CV, 995 seeds × 5 depths)
- **Rewriting**: 0.74 F1
- **Elaboration**: 0.73 F1
- Cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO): Average F1 = 0.753
- Feature drift rates by prompt type:
  - Continuation: 16.9% per depth step (strongest drift)
  - Rewriting: 6.1% per depth step
  - Elaboration: 1.4% per depth step

**2. appendix_human_edit.tex** - Word Replacement Robustness at 10% Degradation
- **At 10% word replacement**: Binary F1 = 0.614 (down from 0.695 baseline)
- **Degradation magnitude**: -8.1 pp total (0.695 → 0.614)
  - 0% → 5%: -4.6 pp (largest marginal drop)
  - 5% → 10%: -3.6 pp (sublinear degradation)
  - 10% → 20%: -2.3 pp
- **3-class F1 at 10% replacement**: 0.492 (down from 0.737, -24.5 pp)
- LLM-simulated sentence regeneration: -4.5 pp (nearly identical to 5% random replacement)

**3. appendix_9gen_scale.tex** - 9-Generator F1 Values
- **Per-generator 3-class LOGO F1** (ranked):
  - Pythia-1B: 0.857 [0.848, 0.866]
  - Qwen3-0.6B: 0.819 [0.807, 0.830]
  - Pythia-2.8B: 0.812 [0.801, 0.822]
  - Qwen3-1.7B: 0.811 [0.800, 0.823]
  - OLMo-2-1B: 0.773 [0.762, 0.784]
  - Llama-3.2-3B: 0.759 [0.748, 0.770]
  - Qwen3-8B: 0.696 [0.683, 0.709]
  - Gemma-2-2B: 0.628 [0.622, 0.634] (anomaly: gen-1 F1 = 0.036)
  - Mistral-7B: 0.605 [0.593, 0.617]
- **9-gen average**: 0.751 (3-class), 0.666 (binary)
- **8-gen average**: 0.770 (3-class), 0.701 (binary)
- 14B-scale evidence (Qwen3-14B): Cohen's d = 0.78–1.12 for drifting features, DDR partition preserved

**4. appendix_decoding_strategy.tex** - DDR Partition Preservation Across Strategies
- **Shape features (near-stationary across all strategies):**
  - Mean Cohen's d under nucleus: 0.08 (entropy_max), 0.04 (entropy_min), 0.13 (tail_decay)
  - Mean Cohen's d under greedy: 0.06, 0.04, 0.06
  - Mean Cohen's d under top-k: 0.10, 0.04, 0.16
  - All values remain below stationarity threshold (d < 0.15) across 8 generators
- **Drifting features (large effects maintained):**
  - Mean Cohen's d under nucleus: 1.09 (entropy_mean), 0.85 (TTR), 0.76 (hapax_ratio), 0.46 (entropy_std)
  - Mean Cohen's d under greedy: 1.16, 0.85, 0.80, 0.83
  - Mean Cohen's d under top-k: 0.79, 0.63, 0.59, 0.23
- **Confirmation**: DDR location/shape partition replicates across all three decoding strategies; top-k partially attenuates but remains recognizable

**5. appendix_classifier.tex** - Ordinal Classifier Comparisons
- **6-generator LOGO across classifiers (3-class F1):**
  - XGBoost: 0.789 average
  - Random Forest: 0.790 average
  - Logistic Regression: 0.725 average
- **Per-generator F1 (XGBoost)**: Pythia-1B (0.856), Py-2.8B (0.804), Qw-0.6B (0.824), Qw-1.7B (0.806), OLMo-2 (0.764), Qw-8B (0.683)
- **Ordinal methods claim**: CORAL and Frank-Hall achieve comparable F1 with overlapping confidence intervals, confirming 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses
- **Hyperparameter sensitivity**: XGBoost max_depth variation yields only 2.0 pp range (0.752–0.772 for 3-class)

**Summary of Key Claims Verified:**
- 0.781 (0.78) continuation F1 ✓ confirmed in prompt_type
- 8 pp degradation at 10% word replacement (8.1 pp to be precise: 0.695→0.614) ✓ confirmed in human_edit
- DDR partition preserved across decoding strategies ✓ confirmed in decoding_strategy
- CORAL/Frank-Hall F1 comparability ✓ confirmed in classifier
```

From introduction.tex I read earlier:
```
24	Our contributions:
25	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
26	    \item ...

```

So it's right after the contributions list. That's essentially option 2. But the user's template says:
"Code and data are available at\footnote{...}"

The current text says:
"Code and data are publicly available.\footnote{...}"

Two issues:
1. The user's template uses "available at" (with "at" linking to the footnote), the current text uses "publicly available." with a period and then a footnote. The user's template has better phrasing where "at" directly precedes the footnote link.
2. The word "publicly" is in the current version but not in the template.

The `\noindent` is fine after an enumerate. Now I need to figure out where this actually falls in the document. The contributions list comes after Figure 1 at the top of the page, and since the introduction spans pages 1-2, this placement is likely on page 2. But the user specified they want a "第一页脚注" (first page footnote), and their preferred options are either at the end of the abstract or in the first paragraph of the introduction where contributions are mentioned. This current placement at the end of the contributions list doesn't match either preference—it's too late in the document.

Since the abstract is on page 1, moving the footnote there would guarantee it appears on the first page as the user wants. I'll remove it from the introduction file and add it to the abstract instead. After that, I should check the GitHub deploy key configuration.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011ffnygcb2bNzypf5k4nNTc`</summary>

```text
1	% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	As large language model (LLM) outputs re-enter training corpora and seed further generation, understanding how recursive generation reshapes text distributions is critical for data provenance and corpus curation---yet existing detectors answer only the binary human-or-machine question, ignoring \emph{generation depth}: we introduce \emph{depth profiling} to address this gap.
4	We decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, negative log-likelihood (NLL) location features drift monotonically with depth (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.46$)---a ${\geq}8{\times}$ effect-size gap.
5	Prior work documents \emph{what converges}; the central finding here is \emph{what is approximately preserved}: shape stability complements location drift for ordinal depth grading without per-generator calibration, achieving leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+), cross-model binary F1\,=\,0.830, and 3-class F1\,=\,0.762 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation (gen-0 seeds shared across generators; \S\ref{sec:generation}).
6	Instruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models.
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```

</details>

    "newString": "Instruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models. We release our code and data at\\footnote{\\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/gen-depth-fingerprint}}.\n\\end{abstract}",
    "originalFile": "% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains\n\\begin{abstract}\nAs large language model (LLM) outputs re-enter training corpora and seed further generation, understanding how recursive generation reshapes text distributions is critical for data provenance and corpus curation---yet existing detectors answer only the binary human-or-machine question, ignoring \\emph{generation depth}: we introduce \\emph{depth profiling} to address this gap.\nWe decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, negative log-likelihood (NLL) location features drift monotonically with depth (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.46$)---a ${\\geq}8{\\times}$ effect-size gap.\nPrior work documents \\emph{what converges}; the central finding here is \\emph{what is approximately preserved}: shape stability complements location drift for ordinal depth grading without per-generator calibration, achieving leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+), cross-model binary F1\\,=\\,0.830, and 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.762 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation (gen-0 seeds shared across generators; \\S\\ref{sec:generation}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "d1a72e7c-470b-43e0-a132-4efcc7192543",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "\\end{enumerate}\n\\noindent Code and data are publicly available.\\footnote{\\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/gen-depth-fingerprint}}",
    "newString": "\\end{enumerate}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1 An estimated 7--17\\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}. As these outputs re-enter training corpora \\cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \\emph{generation depths}; each successive cycle reshapes distributional properties in ways that matter for data provenance, corpus auditing, and contamination monitoring in base-model pretraining corpora \\cite{ccnet,dsir} as well as model collapse analysis \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}. Understanding \\emph{how} recursive generation alters text distributions---and which properties change versus which are preserved---is therefore a prerequisite for principled corpus curation, yet current methods address only binary human-or-machine detection \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.\nBase models provide the cleanest substrate to isolate depth effects from alignment-induced changes.\nWe find that recursive generation produces a decomposable depth profile: distributional \\emph{location} drifts with depth while \\emph{shape} remains stable across generators, an asymmetry sufficient for cross-generator depth prediction.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Distributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \\cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, yet existing analyses characterize \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved.\nWe introduce \\emph{depth profiling} (coarse ordinal classification into gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+), and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy\\footnote{Throughout, ``entropy'' denotes per-token NLL under the reference model (Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \\S\\ref{sec:features}.}, lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains ${\\geq}8{\\times}$ more stable (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$ vs.\\ $d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features; entropy std is intermediate ($d{=}0.46$); boundary features show additional nuance; convergent effect-size measures confirm this partition).\nWe term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.\nThe stationary shape component complements location drift for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \\textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\\rho| > 0.59$). \\textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (red for drifting, blue for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\nWe focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:framework} summarizes the pipeline: recursive generation chains (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), feature extraction with DDR partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:features}), and leave-one-generator-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA \\cite{qlora} (Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation) classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into two primary response zones---drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) vs.\\ near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---plus one intermediate feature ($d{=}0.46$). Where prior work documents \\emph{what converges} \\cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \\emph{what is approximately preserved}, an empirical regularity with analytical utility for depth classification without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B; exploratory evidence extends to 14B, Appendix~\\ref{app:9gen-scale}), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.\n    \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\\noindent Code and data are publicly available.\\footnote{\\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/gen-depth-fingerprint}}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          "     \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.",

Title: \"Generation Depth Fingerprinting: Profiling Recursive LLM Output\"\n   - 简要说明项目（1-2 段）\n   - Environment setup（Python 3.10+, pip install -r requirements.txt）\n   - 数据准备：说明用 Wikipedia seeds，如何用 extract_features.py 提取特征\n   - <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HDHgEKBGbEMP9MYsrwC2S9`</summary>

```text
1	\vspace{-1mm}
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	
5	\looseness=-1 DDR reflects monotonic NLL compression under recursive generation (Appendix~\ref{app:nll-distribution}).
6	We hypothesize that shape stability arises in part from architectural constraints and distributional geometry, though it is not trivially predictable: tail\_decay lacks an obvious architectural bound yet remains near-stationary ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$); entropy\_max is bounded by $\log|V|{\approx}10.8$ nats ($|V|{=}50{,}257$), but observed values fall far below this ceiling---the bound constrains range without explaining within-bound stability; and entropy\_std---also NLL-derived---drifts substantially ($d{=}0.46$), showing NLL derivation alone does not confer stationarity.
7	ANOVA confirms depth explains ${>}48{\times}$ more variance in location than shape features (Appendix~\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Relationship to model collapse.}
10	\looseness=-1 Model collapse erodes tail coverage via weight updates---and shape features \emph{do} change under retraining \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}---whereas DDR characterizes fixed-parameter inference where shape remains stable.
11	Shape stability is thus specific to the fixed-parameter regime, not an a priori consequence of architecture; DDR complements concurrent iterative-generation studies (Appendix~\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Temperature sensitivity.}
14	\looseness=-1 Higher sampling temperature ($\tau > 0.7$) flattens token probability distributions, narrowing NLL variance and diluting the location shift that drifting features capture---the mechanism through which DDR signal degrades at elevated temperatures (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
15	The DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across all tested temperatures (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), but signal strength is temperature-dependent, bounding current applicability to moderate sampling.
16	
17	\paragraph{Error structure.}
18	\looseness=-1 The gen-1$\to$gen-2+ boundary is the primary failure point (error rate 48.4\%; Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}): gen-1 texts retain sufficient diversity to overlap with gen-0; deeper generations show monotonically decreasing error ($\leq$29.2\%), so DDR-based classifiers are most reliable for heavily recycled content.
19	
20	\paragraph{Scope and applications.}
21	\looseness=-1 The DDR partition is robust across decoding strategies, temperatures, and reference models (Appendices~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}--\ref{app:cross-ref}).
22	Instruction tuning compresses DDR (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), but QLoRA recovery to F1\,=\,0.89--0.91 (within-model) confirms the signal persists; cross-generator instruct evaluation remains future work.
23	Cross-domain transfer retains F1\,=\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}); a 9-generator evaluation confirms generalization beyond the original pool (Appendix~\ref{app:9gen-scale}).
24	DDR enables corpus contamination detection and synthetic data pipeline auditing; depth-aware filtering does not improve perplexity (Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}), confirming DDR identifies \emph{provenance} rather than \emph{fluency degradation}.
25	Finer-grained 5-class classification yields F1\,$\approx$\,0.50 (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}), suggesting a resolution limit of three depth classes.
26	
```

      "content": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\looseness=-1 DDR reflects monotonic NLL compression under recursive generation (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}).\nWe hypothesize that shape stability arises in part from architectural constraints and distributional geometry, though it is not trivially predictable: tail\\_decay lacks an obvious architectural bound yet remains near-stationary ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$); entropy\\_max is bounded by $\\log|V|{\\approx}10.8$ nats ($|V|{=}50{,}257$), but observed values fall far below this ceiling---the bound constrains range without explaining within-bound stability; and entropy\\_std---also NLL-derived---drifts substantially ($d{=}0.46$), showing NLL derivation alone does not confer stationarity.\nANOVA confirms depth explains ${>}48{\\times}$ more variance in location than shape features (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to model collapse.}\n\\looseness=-1 Model collapse erodes tail coverage via weight updates---and shape features \\emph{do} change under retraining \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}---whereas DDR characterizes fixed-parameter inference where shape remains stable.\nShape stability is thus specific to the fixed-parameter regime, not an a priori consequence of architecture; DDR complements concurrent iterative-generation studies (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature sensitivity.}\n\\looseness=-1 Higher sampling temperature ($\\tau > 0.7$) flattens token probability distributions, narrowing NLL variance and diluting the location shift that drifting features capture---the mechanism through which DDR signal degrades at elevated temperatures (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nThe DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across all tested temperatures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), but signal strength is temperature-dependent, bounding current applicability to moderate sampling.\n\n\\paragraph{Error structure.}\n\\looseness=-1 The gen-1$\\to$gen-2+ boundary is the primary failure point (error rate 48.4\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}): gen-1 texts retain sufficient diversity to overlap with gen-0; deeper generations show monotonically decreasing error ($\\leq$29.2\\%), so DDR-based classifiers are most reliable for heavily recycled content.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and applications.}\n\\looseness=-1 The DDR partition is robust across decoding strategies, temperatures, and reference models (Appendices~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}--\\ref{app:cross-ref}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), but QLoRA recovery to F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91 (within-model) confirms the signal persists; cross-generator instruct evaluation remains future work.\nCross-domain transfer retains F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}); a 9-generator evaluation confirms generalization beyond the original pool (Appendix~\\ref{app:9gen-scale}).\nDDR enables corpus contamination detection and synthetic data pipeline auditing; 134	\looseness=-1 We next ask whether existing detection methods---designed for binary real-vs-synthetic classification---incidentally capture depth structure, repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
135	The strongest baseline is a 4-detector + surface feature ensemble (binary F1\,=\,0.708), which pools four detection scores with TTR and hapax ratio; among individual detectors, Avg-LogProb provides the most meaningful single-feature comparison.
136	
137	\looseness=-1 The 7-feature ensemble matches the 4-detector+surface ensemble on binary F1 (0.705 vs.\ 0.708; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost, though the latter leads by 2.1\,pp on 3-class F1 (0.795 vs.\ 0.774); both surpass individual detectors by 3.0--16.6\,pp.
138	The DDR decomposition explains why depth discrimination is achievable---location features carry the signal, shape features enable cross-generator transfer (\S\ref{sec:discussion})---with a single 410M reference model; ordinal-specific methods (CORAL, Frank-Hall) yield comparable F1 (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}).
139	Among individual detectors, LD-Score and Avg-LogProb best capture depth structure (binary F1\,=\,0.675 and 0.673); FastDetectGPT captures 3-class ordering (F1\,=\,0.660) but struggles with binary depth separation (F1\,=\,0.569); Binoculars is least informative (binary F1\,=\,0.539).
140	Concurrent iterative-generation studies use different chain operators, precluding direct inclusion as depth baselines (Appendix~\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).
